) (by using divergence theorem and integration by parts in time variable). Here we only list the highest-order terms. We have

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}[\mathring{\mathbf{Q}}]^{(n)}[\partial_{t}^{3},\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}^{\cdot},v^{(n-1)}]\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}+\int_{\Omega}[\mathring{\mathbf{Q}}]^{(n)}\mathfrak{C}_{i}^{(n-1)}(v_{i}^{(n)})\mathrm{d}\mathcal{V}_{t}^{(n)}

(5.102)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\partial_{3}^{\widetilde{\varphi}^{(n)}}[\mathring{\mathbf{Q}}]^{(n)}[\partial_{t}^{3},\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}^{\cdot},v^{(n-1)}]\mathrm{d}\mathcal{V}_{t}^{(n)}+\text{ controllable terms},

and thus it can be controlled under time integral:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{3}^{\widetilde{\varphi}^{(n)}}[\mathring{\mathbf{Q}}]^{(n)}[\partial_{t}^{3},\widetilde{\mathbf{N}}^{\cdot},v^{(n-1)}]\mathrm{d}\mathcal{V}_{t}^{(n)}\,\mathrm{d}t\lesssim

\displaystyle~\varepsilon\|\partial_{t}^{2}[\check{q}]^{(n)}\|_{1}^{2}+C(\mathring{K}_{0})\left([\mathring{E}]^{(n)}(0)+\int_{0}^{T}[\mathring{E}]^{(n)}(t)+[\mathring{E}]^{(n-1)}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right).

(5.103)

Combining the above analysis and using the definition of Alinhac good unknowns, we get

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{3}[v]^{(n)}(t)\|_{0}^{2}+\|\sqrt{\mathcal{F}^{{(n)}^{\prime}}}(q^{(n)})\partial_{t}^{3}[\check{q}]^{(n)}(t)\|_{0}^{2}+\kappa^{2}\int_{0}^{t}|\partial_{t}^{4}\psi(\tau)|_{1}^{2}\mathrm{d}\tau

(5.104)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\varepsilon\|\partial_{t}^{2}[\check{q}]^{(n)}\|_{1}^{2}+C(\mathring{K}_{0},\kappa^{-1})\left([\mathring{E}]^{(n)}(0)+\int_{0}^{T}[\mathring{E}]^{(n)}(t)+[\mathring{E}]^{(n-1)}(t)+[\mathring{E}]^{(n-2)}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right).

5.4
Well-posedness of the nonlinear
\kappa
-approximate problem

Combining the div-curl analysis, the control of the normal traces, the reduction of
[\check{q}]
and the analysis of full-time derivatives for the linear system (
5.80
) for
[v]^{(n)},[\check{q}]^{(n)},[\psi]^{(n)}
, we arrive at the energy estimate:

[\mathring{E}]^{(n)}(t)\leq C(\mathring{K}_{0},\kappa^{-1})\left([\mathring{E}]^{(n)}(0)+\int_{0}^{T}[\mathring{E}]^{(n)}(t)+[\mathring{E}]^{(n-1)}(t)+[\mathring{E}]^{(n-2)}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\right).

(5.105)

Since
[v]^{(n)},[\check{q}]^{(n)},[\psi]^{(n)}
have zero initial data, one can repeatedly use (
5.80
) to show that their time derivatives also vanish on
\{t=0\}
, as one can observe that every term in the first two equations of (
5.80
) contains exactly one term involving
[f]^{(n)}
or
[f]^{(n-1)}
whose initial value is zero. This implies
[\mathring{E}]^{(n)}(0)=0
, and thus there exists some
T_{1}^{\kappa}>0
independent of
n
, such that

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T_{1}^{\kappa}}[\mathring{E}]^{{(n)}}(t)\leq\frac{1}{4}\left(\sup_{0\leq t\leq T_{1}^{\kappa}}[\mathring{E}]^{{(n-1)}}(t)+\sup_{0\leq t\leq T_{1}^{\kappa}}[\mathring{E}]^{{(n-2)}}(t)\right),

(5.106)

and thus we know by induction that

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T_{1}^{\kappa}}[\mathring{E}]^{{(n)}}(t)\leq C(\mathring{K}_{0},\kappa^{-1})/2^{n-1}\to 0\text{ as }n\to+\infty.

(5.107)

Hence, for any fixed
\kappa>0
, the sequence of approximate solutions
\{(v^{(n)},\check{q}^{(n)},\rho^{(n)},\psi^{(n)})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
has a strongly convergent subsequence, whose limit
(v^{\kappa},\check{q}^{\kappa},\rho^{\kappa},\psi^{\kappa})
is exactly the solution to the nonlinear
\kappa
-problem (
3.11
). The uniqueness follows from a parallel argument.

6
Well-posedness and incompressible limit of the gravity(-capillary) water wave system

We are ready to prove the local existence of the original water wave system (
1.24
) for each fixed
\sigma>0
. In Section
5
, we prove the local well-posedness and higher-order energy estimates of the linearized system (
5.6
) for each
fixed

\kappa>0
and use Picard iteration to construct the unique strong solution to the nonlinear
\kappa
-approximate problem (
3.11
) defined in Section
3.2
. To pass the limit
\kappa\to 0_{+}
to the original system (
1.24
), we prove the uniform-in-
\kappa
estimates for (
3.11
) in Section
4
. Therefore, we prove the local-in-time existence for the stronger solution to the compressible gravity-capillary water wave system (
1.24
), that is, given initial data
(v_{0},\rho_{0},\psi_{0})
, there exists
T^{\prime}>0
only depending on the initial data, such that the original system (
1.24
) has a solution
(v,\rho,\psi)
satisfying the energy estimates

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T^{\prime}}E(t)\leq P(E(0)).

(6.1)

6.1
Uniqueness

To prove the well-posedness, it suffices to prove the uniqueness of the solution to (
1.24
). We assume

\{(v^{(n)},\check{q}^{(n)},\rho^{(n)},\psi^{(n)})\}_{n=1,2}

to be two solutions to (
1.24
) and define
[f]=f^{(1)}-f^{(2)}
for any function
f
. Then it suffices to prove
([v],[\check{q}],[\rho],[\psi])=(\mathbf{0},0,0,0)
. We find that
([v],[\check{q}],[\rho],[\psi])=(\mathbf{0},0,0,0)
satisfies the following system:

\begin{cases}\rho^{(1)}D_{t}^{\varphi^{(1)}}[v]+\nabla^{\varphi^{(1)}}[\check{q}]=-f_{v}&~~~\text{ in }[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(q^{(1)})D_{t}^{\varphi^{(1)}}[\check{q}]+\nabla^{\varphi^{(1)}}\cdot[v]=-f_{q}&~~~\text{ in }[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
[\check{q}]=[q]+g[\varphi]&~~~\text{ in }[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
[\check{q}]=g[\psi]-\sigma\left(\mathcal{H}(\overline{\nabla}\psi^{(1)})-\mathcal{H}(\overline{\nabla}\psi^{(2)})\right)&~~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma,\\
\partial_{t}[\psi]=[v]\cdot N^{(1)}+v^{(2)}\cdot[N]&~~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma,\\
[v_{3}]=0&~~~\text{ on }[0,T]\times\Sigma_{b},\\
([v],[\check{q}],[\psi])|_{t=0}=(\mathbf{0},0,0)\end{cases}

(6.2)

where the functions
f_{v},f_{q}
are defined by

\displaystyle f_{v}

\displaystyle:=[\rho]\partial_{t}v^{(2)}+[\rho\overline{v}]\cdot\overline{\nabla}v^{(2)}+[\rho V_{\mathbf{N}}]\partial_{3}v^{(2)}+\rho^{(2)}ge_{3}+\partial_{3}\check{q}^{(2)}[A_{i3}]

(6.3)

\displaystyle f_{q}

\displaystyle:=[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(q)](\partial_{t}\check{q}^{(2)}-gv_{3}^{(2)})+[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(q)\overline{v}]\cdot\overline{\nabla}\check{q}^{(2)}+[\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(q)V_{\mathbf{N}}]\partial_{3}q^{(2)}

(6.4)

\displaystyle-\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(q^{(2)})g[v_{3}]+\partial_{3}v_{i}^{(2)}[A_{i3}],

and

\displaystyle V_{\mathbf{N}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi),~A_{13}:=-\frac{\partial_{1}\varphi}{\partial_{3}\varphi},~A_{23}:=-\frac{\partial_{2}\varphi}{\partial_{3}\varphi},~A_{33}:=\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi},

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(\overline{\nabla}\psi)

\displaystyle:=\overline{\nabla}\cdot\left(\frac{\overline{\nabla}\psi}{|N|}\right),~~\mathcal{H}(\overline{\nabla}\psi^{(1)})-\mathcal{H}(\overline{\nabla}\psi^{(2)})=\overline{\nabla}\cdot\left(\frac{\overline{\nabla}[\psi]}{|N^{(1)}|}-\left(\frac{1}{|N^{(1)}|}-\frac{1}{|N^{(2)}|}\right)\overline{\nabla}\psi^{(2)}\right).

Let

[E](t):=\sum_{k=0}^{3}\|\partial_{t}^{k}[v]\|_{3-k}^{2}+\sigma|\overline{\nabla}\partial_{t}^{k}[\psi]|_{3-k}^{2}+g|[\psi]|_{0}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{2}\|\partial_{t}^{k}[\check{q}]\|_{3-k}^{2}+\|\sqrt{\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(q^{(1)})}\partial_{t}^{3}[\check{q}]\|_{0}^{2}.

(6.5)

We can then mimic the proof for the uniform-in-
\kappa
estimates (setting
\kappa=0
) in Section
4
to show that
[E](0)=0
and
[E](t)
satisfies the following energy inequality

[E](T)\leq\int_{0}^{T}P(E(t))[E](t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

(6.6)

Here, compared with the process of Picard iteration, the only difference is that the boundary integral produces some extra terms that are controlled using mollification before, and we must use the surface tension instead of the artificial viscosity term to produce the boundary regularity. Following the analysis in Section
5.3.3
, the main contribution of the boundary integral arising from
\overline{\partial}{}^{3}
-tangential estimates is

-\int_{\Sigma}[\mathbf{Q}][\mathbf{V}]\cdot N^{(1)}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{=}}-\int_{\Sigma}\overline{\partial}{}^{3}[q]\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}{}^{3}[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}+\int_{\Sigma}\overline{\partial}{}^{3}[q]\overline{\partial}{}^{3}(v^{(2)}\cdot[N])\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime},

(6.7)

where
[\mathbf{Q}],[\mathbf{V}]
are the Alinhac good unknowns of
[\check{q}],[v]
with respect to
\overline{\partial}{}^{3}
and
\varphi^{(1)}
, that is,
[\mathbf{F}]:=\mathbf{F}^{(1)}-\mathbf{F}^{(2)}
.
For the first integral, we have

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\overline{\partial}{}^{3}[q]\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}{}^{3}[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Sigma}|N^{(1)}|^{-1}\left|\overline{\partial}{}^{3}\overline{\nabla}[\psi]\right|_{0}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\sigma{\int_{\Sigma}\frac{\overline{\partial}{}^{3}\overline{\nabla}[\psi]\cdot\overline{\nabla}(\psi^{(1)}+\psi^{(2)})}{|N^{(1)}||N^{(2)}|(|N^{(1)}|+|N^{(2)}|)}\overline{\nabla}\psi^{(2)}\cdot\partial_{t}\overline{\nabla}\overline{\partial}{}^{3}[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime},}

(6.8)

where the first term gives the boundary energy in
[E](t)
, and the second term appears when
\overline{\partial}{}^{3}
falls on

|N^{(1)}|^{-1}-|N^{(2)}|^{-1}=\frac{|N^{(2)}|^{2}-|N^{(1)}|^{2}}{|N^{(1)}||N^{(2)}|(|N^{(1)}+|N^{(2)}|)}.

This term is controlled by

\displaystyle-\sigma\int_{\Sigma}\frac{\overline{\partial}{}^{3}\overline{\nabla}[\psi]\cdot\overline{\nabla}(\psi^{(1)}+\psi^{(2)})}{|N^{(1)}||N^{(2)}|(|N^{(1)}|+|N^{(2)}|)}\overline{\nabla}\psi^{(2)}\cdot\partial_{t}\overline{\nabla}\overline{\partial}{}^{3}[\psi]\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~P(|\overline{\nabla}\psi^{(1)},\overline{\nabla}\psi^{(2)}|_{\infty})|\sqrt{\sigma}\overline{\nabla}\overline{\partial}{}^{3}[\psi]|_{0}(|\sqrt{\sigma}\partial_{t}\psi^{(1)}|_{4}+|\sqrt{\sigma}\partial_{t}\psi^{(2)}|_{4})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle~\varepsilon|\sqrt{\sigma}\overline{\nabla}\overline{\partial}{}^{3}[\psi]|_{0}^{2}+P(|\overline{\nabla}\psi^{(1)},\overline{\nabla}\psi^{(2)}|_{\infty})E(t)\leq\varepsilon[E](t)+P(E(t)).

The energy inequality for
[E](t)
together with Grönwall’s inequality and the energy bounds for
E(t)
implies that there exists some
T\in[0,T^{\prime}]
only depending on the initial data of (
1.24
), such that
\sup\limits_{0\leq t\leq T}[E](t)\leq 2[E](0)=0
. Therefore, the solution to (
6.2
) must be zero. The uniqueness is proven, and the continuous dependence on initial data in
H^{3}(\Omega)
for
v,\check{q}
and in
H^{4}(\Sigma)
for
\psi
is similarly proven.

6.2
Incompressible and zero-surface-tension limits

This section is devoted to showing that we can pass the solution of (
1.24
) to the incompressible and zero surface tension double limits. In other words, we study the behavior of the solution of (
1.24
) as both the Mach number
\lambda
and surface tension coefficient
\sigma
tend to
0
. Recall that the Mach number
\lambda
is defined in Section
1.3
.

We study the incompressible Euler equations modeling the motion of incompressible gravity water waves without surface tension satisfied by
(\xi,w,q_{in})
with initial data
(w_{0},\xi_{0})
and
w_{0}^{3}|_{\Sigma_{b}}=0
:

\begin{cases}D_{t}^{\varphi}w+\nabla^{\varphi}\mathfrak{p}=0&~~~\text{in}~[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot w=0&~~~\text{in}~[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
\mathfrak{p}=q_{in}+g\varphi&~~~\text{in}~[0,T]\times\Omega,\\
\mathfrak{p}=g\xi&~~~\text{on}~[0,T]\times\Sigma,\\
\partial_{t}\xi=w\cdot\mathcal{N}&~~~\text{on}~[0,T]\times\Sigma,\\
w_{3}=0&~~~\text{on}~[0,T]\times\Sigma_{b},\\
(w,\xi)|_{t=0}=(w_{0},\xi_{0}),\end{cases}

(6.9)

where we define
\varphi(t,x)=x_{3}+\chi(x_{3})\xi(t,x^{\prime})
to be the extension of
\xi
in
\Omega
after slightly abuse of notations.
Denote by

(\psi^{\lambda,\sigma},v^{\lambda,\sigma},\rho^{\lambda,\sigma})

the solution of (
1.24
)
indexed by
\lambda
and
\sigma
, our goal is to show:

(\psi^{\lambda,\sigma},v^{\lambda,\sigma},\rho^{\lambda,\sigma})\to(\xi,w,1)\quad\text{in}\,\,C^{0}([0,T];{}{H_{\text{loc}}^{4-\delta}(\Sigma)}\times H_{\text{loc}}^{4-\delta}(\Omega)\times H_{\text{loc}}^{3-\delta}(\Omega)),\quad\text{for any}\,\,\delta\in(0,1],

(6.10)

provided that:

1.

The sequence of initial data
(\psi_{0}^{\lambda,\sigma},v_{0}^{\lambda,\sigma},\rho_{0}^{\lambda,\sigma}-1)\in H^{5}(\Sigma)\times H^{4}(\Omega)\times H^{4}(\Omega)
satisfies the compatibility conditions up to order
3
,
|\psi_{0}^{\lambda,\sigma}|_{\infty}\leq 1
, and
v_{0}^{3;{\lambda,\sigma}}|_{\Sigma_{b}}=0
. The compatibility condition of order
k
(
k\geq 0
), expressed in terms of the modified pressure, reads

\displaystyle(D_{t}^{\varphi})^{k}\check{q}^{{\lambda,\sigma}}|_{\{t=0\}\times\Sigma}=\sigma(D_{t}^{\varphi})^{k}\left(\mathcal{H}^{{\lambda,\sigma}}+g\psi^{{\lambda,\sigma}}\right)|_{\{t=0\}\times\Sigma}.

(6.11)

Since
D_{t}^{\varphi}=\partial_{t}+\overline{v}^{{\lambda,\sigma}}\cdot\overline{\partial}{}
on
\Sigma
, we can rewrite (
6.11
) as:

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}+\overline{v}^{{\lambda,\sigma}}\cdot\overline{\partial}{})^{k}\check{q}^{{\lambda,\sigma}}|_{t=0}=\sigma(\partial_{t}+\overline{v}^{{\lambda,\sigma}}\cdot\overline{\partial}{})^{k}\left(\mathcal{H}^{{\lambda,\sigma}}+g\psi^{{\lambda,\sigma}}\right)|_{t=0}\quad\text{on}\,\,\,\Sigma.

(6.12)

Apart from this, we require

\partial_{t}^{k}v^{3;{\lambda,\sigma}}|_{\{t=0\}\times\Sigma_{b}}=0,\quad k=0,1,2,3,

(6.13)

The existence of such data is discussed in Appendix
B
.

2.

(\psi_{0}^{\lambda,\sigma},v_{0}^{\lambda,\sigma},\rho_{0}^{\lambda,\sigma})\to(\xi,w,1)
in
{}{H^{4}(\Sigma)}\times H^{4}(\Omega)\times H^{3}(\Omega)
as
{\lambda,\sigma}\to 0
.

3.

The compressible pressure
q^{{\lambda,\sigma}}
and the incompressible pressure
q_{in}
satisfy the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition:

\displaystyle-\partial_{3}q^{{\lambda,\sigma}}\geq c_{0}>0,\quad\text{on}\,\,\{t=0\}\times\Sigma,

(6.14)

\displaystyle-\partial_{3}q_{in}\geq c_{0}>0,\quad\text{on}\,\,\{t=0\}\times\Sigma.

(6.15)

The key step of showing the
{\lambda,\sigma}
-double limits is to prove an energy estimate of (
1.24
) that is uniform in both
\lambda
and
\sigma
. The analysis in Section
4
indicates that the energy estimate for (
4.1
) is already uniform in
\lambda
. In particular, one can see that the tangential energy estimates in Sections
4.5
-
4.6
are uniform in
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\lambda}
, which is of size
O(\lambda^{2})
by (
1.29
).

The energy bound that we obtained from the local existence implies the boundedness of
\|\partial_{t}^{k}v^{\lambda,\sigma}(t)\|_{4-k}^{2}+|\partial_{t}^{k}\psi^{\lambda,\sigma}(t)|_{4-k}^{2}~(k\leq 4)
uniformly in both
\lambda
and
\sigma
within the time interval
[0,T]
. Thus,

\displaystyle(v^{{\lambda,\sigma}},\psi^{{\lambda,\sigma}})\rightarrow(w,\xi),\quad\text{ as }{\lambda,\sigma}\to 0,

(6.16)

weakly-* in
L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{4}(\Omega)\times{}{H^{4}(\Sigma)})
, and strongly in
C^{0}([0,T];H_{\text{loc}}^{4-\delta}(\Omega)\times{}{H_{\text{loc}}^{4-\delta}(\Sigma)})
for any
0<\delta\leq 1
. Here, the strong convergence is a direct consequence of the Aubin-Lions lemma, and the uniqueness of the limit function implies the convergence without squeezing a subsequence.

Moreover, as
D_{t}^{\varphi}=\partial_{t}+(\overline{v}\cdot\overline{\nabla})+(\partial_{3}\varphi)^{-1}(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi)\partial_{3}
,
invoking the continuity equation

\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(q)D_{t}^{\varphi}\check{q}^{\lambda,\sigma}+\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot v^{\lambda,\sigma}=g\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(q)D_{t}^{\varphi}v_{3}^{\lambda,\sigma},

and because
\|D_{t}^{\varphi}\check{q}^{\lambda,\sigma}(t)\|_{3},\|D_{t}^{\varphi}v^{\lambda,\sigma}(t)\|_{3}
are uniformly bounded in
[0,T]
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot v^{\lambda,\sigma}\rightarrow\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot w=0,

(6.17)

weakly-* in
L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{3}(\Omega))
, and strongly in
C^{0}([0,T];H_{\text{loc}}^{3-\delta}(\Omega))
. Once again, the strong convergence is obtained thanks to the Aubin-Lions lemma.

Finally, since the continuity equation can be expressed as

D_{t}^{\varphi}(\rho^{\lambda,\sigma}-1)+\rho^{\lambda,\sigma}(\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot v^{\lambda,\sigma})=0,

we can derive the energy estimate for
\rho^{\lambda,\sigma}-1
in
H^{3}(\Omega)
as:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}\|\rho^{\lambda,\sigma}-1\|_{3}^{2}\leq\|\rho^{\lambda,\sigma}-1\|_{0}\Big(\|v^{\lambda,\sigma}\|_{4}+|\overline{\partial}{}\psi^{{\lambda,\sigma}}|_{3}\Big),

(6.18)

where
\|v^{\lambda,\sigma}\|_{4}
,
|\overline{\partial}{}\psi^{{\lambda,\sigma}}|_{3}
are bounded by
E^{\lambda,\sigma}
. Similarly, we can prove the uniform bound also for
\|\partial_{t}(\rho^{{\lambda,\sigma}}-1)\|_{2}^{2}
.
Therefore,
\rho^{{\lambda,\sigma}}\rightarrow 1
weakly* in
L^{\infty}([0,T];H^{3}(\Omega)
, and strongly in
C^{0}([0,T];H_{\text{loc}}^{3-\delta}(\Omega))
.

7
Improved incompressible limit for well-prepared initial data

Recall that the uniform boundedness (with respect to Mach number) of top-order time derivatives is required to establish the uniform-in-
({\lambda,\sigma})
estimates in Theorem
1.2
. However, only the boundedness of first-order time derivatives is required, namely
\text{div }v=O(\lambda)
and
\partial_{t}v=O(1)
if the initial data is well-prepared. In this section, we aim to drop the boundedness assumption for high-order time derivatives. Since we also need to guarantee the propagation of the Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition, the uniform boundedness of
\partial_{t}\partial_{3}q\sim\partial_{t}^{2}v
is still required.

It should be noted that there is a new difficulty in the control of the “weaker” energy
\mathfrak{E}(t)
: There exhibits a loss of weight of Mach number in
\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}
-tangential estimates when analyzing
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
. In particular, we have to control the following quantity in the cancellation structure used at the end of Section
4.6
:

\int_{\Omega}(\overline{\partial}{}\partial_{3}\partial_{t}^{2}v_{i})(\overline{\partial}{}\mathbf{N}_{i})(\overline{\partial}{}\partial_{3}\partial_{t}^{2}q)\,\mathrm{d}x,

in which
\partial_{t}^{2}q
has to be uniformly bounded with respect to Mach number. However, now we only have
\partial_{t}^{2}q=O(1/\lambda)
, which leads to a loss of
\lambda
-weight. Besides, similar difficulty also appears in the control of
-\int_{\Omega}\mathbf{V}^{\pm}\cdot\mathfrak{C}(q^{\pm})\mathrm{d}\mathcal{V}_{t}
. Indeed, such loss of
\lambda
-weight necessarily happens in
\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}
-tangential estimates because of the following two reasons

1.

\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}q
needs one more
\lambda
-weight than
\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}v
, and

2.

The (extension of) normal vector
\mathbf{N}
, which arises from the commutator
[\overline{\partial}{}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2},\mathbf{N}_{i}/\partial_{3}\varphi,\partial_{3}f]
in
\mathfrak{C}_{i}(f)
, may NOT absorb a time derivative.

Such loss of weights of Mach number is completely caused by the free-surface motion because the commutator
\mathfrak{C}(f)
is not needed in the fixed-domain setting.
In the second author’s previous work
[
76
]
considering compressible inviscid fluids
without surface tension
, such essential difficulty can be avoided thanks to the vanishing Dirichlet boundary condition
q|_{\Sigma}=0
, but that framework is no longer applicable here. To get rid of the loss of Mach number, we have to find a new way to control
\partial_{t}^{2}v
. We also need to introduce a new energy functional:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}(t):=

\displaystyle~\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)+E_{5}(t),

(7.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t):=

\displaystyle~\|v\|_{4}^{2}+\|\check{q}\|_{4}^{2}+|\sqrt{\sigma}\psi|_{5}^{2}+|\psi|_{4}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}v,\partial_{t}\check{q}\|_{3}^{2}+|\sqrt{\sigma}\partial_{t}\psi|_{4}^{2}+|\partial_{t}\psi|_{3.5}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|\partial_{t}^{2}v,\lambda\partial_{t}^{2}\check{q}\|_{2}^{2}+|\sqrt{\sigma}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{3}^{2}+|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{2.5}^{2}+|\partial_{t}^{3}\psi|_{1.5}^{2}

(7.2)

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=3}^{4}\|\lambda\partial_{t}^{k}(v,\check{q})\|_{4-k}^{2}+|\sqrt{\sigma}\lambda\partial_{t}^{k}\psi|_{5-k}^{2}+|\lambda\partial_{t}^{4}\psi|_{0.5}^{2}

\displaystyle E_{5}(t):=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{5}\left\|\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{k}(v,\lambda^{(k-4)_{+}}\check{q})\right\|_{5-k}^{2}+\left|\sqrt{\sigma}\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi\right|_{6-k}^{2}+\left|\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi\right|_{5-k}^{2}

(7.3)

We now introduce the following div-curl inequality

Lemma 7.1

(Hodge-type elliptic estimates)
.

For any sufficiently smooth vector field
X
and
s\geq 1
, one has

\|X\|_{s}^{2}\lesssim C(|\psi|_{s+\frac{1}{2}},|\overline{\nabla}\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}})\left(\|X\|_{0}^{2}+\|\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot X\|_{s-1}^{2}+\|\nabla^{\varphi}\times X\|_{s-1}^{2}+|X\cdot N|_{s-\frac{1}{2}}^{2}\right),

(7.4)

where the constant
C(|\psi|_{s+\frac{1}{2}},|\overline{\nabla}\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}})>0
depends linearly on
|\psi|_{s+\frac{1}{2}}^{2}
.

Applying this inequality to
X=\partial_{t}^{2}v
and
s=2
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim C(|\psi|_{2.5},|\overline{\nabla}\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}})\left(\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{0}^{2}+\|\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{1}^{2}+\|\nabla^{\varphi}\times\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{1}^{2}+|\partial_{t}^{2}v\cdot N|_{s-\frac{1}{2}}^{2}\right).

(7.5)

The divergence and vorticity are controlled in the same way as in Section
4.3
. As for the boundary term, we have

\partial_{t}^{2}v\cdot N=\partial_{t}^{3}\psi+\overline{v}_{j}\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi,

so we shall turn to control
|\partial_{t}^{3}\psi|_{1.5}
and
|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{2.5}
without any weights of
{\lambda,\sigma}
.

7.1
Time-differentiated evolution equation of the free surface

We derive the evolution equation of the free surface by further differentiating the kinematic boundary condition in time variable.

7.1.1
Time-differentiated kinematic boundary condition

Let
\overline{D_{t}}:=D_{t}^{\varphi}|_{\Sigma}=\partial_{t}+\overline{v}\cdot\overline{\nabla}
. The kinematic boundary condition then implies

\overline{D_{t}}\psi=v_{3},\quad\text{ on }\Sigma.

(7.6)

Taking one more
\overline{D_{t}}
to (
7.6
), we infer from the momentum equation that

\rho\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\psi=-\partial_{3}\check{q}-(\rho-1)g,\quad\text{ on }\Sigma.

(7.7)

Since
[\partial_{t},\overline{D_{t}}]f=\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\overline{\partial}{}_{j}f
, we obtain

\displaystyle[\partial_{t}^{2},\overline{D_{t}}]f=\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{v}_{j}\overline{\partial}{}_{j}f+2\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}{}_{j}f.

From this and
[\overline{\partial}{}_{j},\overline{D_{t}}]=\overline{\partial}{}_{i}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}f
, we see that

\displaystyle[\partial_{t}^{2},\overline{D_{t}}^{2}]f=

\displaystyle~\overline{D_{t}}\left(\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{v}_{j}\overline{\partial}{}_{j}f+2\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}{}_{j}f\right)+\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{D_{t}}f+2\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\partial_{t}\overline{D_{t}}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{D_{t}}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}f-2\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{v}_{k}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{k}f-2\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{v}_{k}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{k}f

\displaystyle+2\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{D_{t}}f+2\partial_{t}\overline{D_{t}}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\partial_{t}f-4\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{v}_{k}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{k}\partial_{t}f

\displaystyle+4\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\partial_{t}\overline{D_{t}}f-2\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}(\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{k}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{k}f).

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\psi=

\displaystyle\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi+\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{D_{t}}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\psi-2\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{v}_{k}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{k}\psi-2\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{v}_{k}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{k}\psi

(7.8)

\displaystyle+2\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{D_{t}}\psi+2\partial_{t}\overline{D_{t}}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\partial_{t}\psi-4\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{v}_{k}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{k}\partial_{t}\psi

\displaystyle+4\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\partial_{t}\overline{D_{t}}\psi-2\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}(\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{k}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{k}\psi).

Combining this with (
7.7
) yields

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi=-\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{t}^{2}\partial_{3}\check{q}-\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{D_{t}}\overline{v}\cdot\overline{\nabla}\psi+\mathbf{R}_{\psi}+\mathbf{R}_{\rho},\quad\text{ on }\Sigma,\end{aligned}

(7.9)

where

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -\mathbf{R}_{\psi}=&-2\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{v}_{k}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{k}\psi-2\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{v}_{k}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{k}\psi+2\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{D_{t}}\psi+2\partial_{t}\overline{D_{t}}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\partial_{t}\psi\\
&-4\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\overline{v}_{k}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{k}\partial_{t}\psi+4\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}\partial_{t}\overline{D_{t}}\psi-2\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{j}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{j}(\partial_{t}\overline{v}_{k}\,\overline{\partial}{}_{k}\psi)\end{aligned}

(7.10)

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}_{\rho}=-\partial_{t}^{2}\left(\frac{(\rho-1)g}{\rho}\right)-\left[\partial_{t}^{2},\frac{1}{\rho}\right]\partial_{3}\check{q}.

(7.11)

For
i=1,2
, since
\rho\overline{D_{t}}v_{i}=-\overline{\partial}^{\varphi}_{i}\check{q}
with
\overline{\partial}^{\varphi}_{i}:=\partial^{\varphi}_{i}|_{\Sigma}=\partial_{i}-\partial_{i}\psi\partial_{3}
, we have

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{D_{t}}\overline{v}\cdot\overline{\nabla}\psi=\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{\partial}^{\varphi}\check{q}\cdot\overline{\nabla}\psi+\underbrace{\left[\partial_{t}^{2},\frac{1}{\rho}\right]\overline{\partial}^{\varphi}\check{q}\cdot\overline{\nabla}\psi}_{=\mathbf{R}_{\psi,\rho}}\quad\text{ on }\Sigma.

(7.12)

Also, since
\overline{\partial}^{\varphi}_{3}:=\partial^{\varphi}_{3}|_{\Sigma}=\partial_{3}
, then

-\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{t}^{2}\partial_{3}\check{q}+\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{\partial}^{\varphi}\check{q}\cdot\overline{\nabla}\psi=-\frac{1}{\rho}N\cdot\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{\partial}^{\varphi}\check{q}\quad\text{ on }\Sigma.

This leads to the following evolution equation of the moving interface:

\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi=-\frac{1}{\rho}N\cdot\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{\partial}^{\varphi}\check{q}+\mathbf{R}_{\psi}+\mathbf{R}_{\rho}+\mathbf{R}_{\psi,\rho},\quad\text{ on }\Sigma,

(7.13)

where
\mathbf{R}_{\psi},\mathbf{R}_{\rho}
, and
\mathbf{R}_{\psi,\rho}
are given respectively in (
7.10
), (
7.11
) and (
7.12
).

7.1.2
The reformulation in Alinhac good unknowns

In the next, we introduce
\mathcal{Q}
to be the Alinhac’s good unknown of
\check{q}
associated with
\partial_{t}^{2}
:

\mathcal{Q}:=\partial_{t}^{2}\check{q}-\partial_{t}^{2}\varphi\partial^{\varphi}_{3}\check{q},\quad\text{ in }\Omega.

(7.14)

For
j=1,2,3
, similar to (
4.36
), we have

\partial_{t}^{2}\nabla^{\varphi}_{j}\check{q}=\nabla^{\varphi}_{j}\mathcal{Q}+C_{j}(\check{q}),\quad\text{ in }\Omega.

(7.15)

Here, for a generic function
f
, we define

\displaystyle C_{i}(f)=\partial^{\varphi}_{i}\partial^{\varphi}_{3}f\partial_{t}^{2}\varphi+C^{\prime}_{i}(f),\quad i=1,2,\text{ and }\quad C_{3}(\check{q})=(\partial^{\varphi}_{3})^{2}f\partial_{t}^{2}\varphi+C^{\prime}_{3}(f),

(7.16)

where

\displaystyle C_{i}^{\prime}(f)=-\left[\partial_{t}^{2},\frac{\partial_{i}\varphi}{\partial_{3}\varphi},\partial_{3}f\right]-\partial_{3}f\left[\partial_{t}^{2},\partial_{i}\varphi,\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}\right]-\partial_{i}\varphi\partial_{3}f\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{(\partial_{3}\varphi)^{2}}\right)\partial_{t}\partial_{3}\varphi,

and

\displaystyle C_{3}^{\prime}(f)=\left[\partial_{t}^{2},\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi},\partial_{3}f\right]+\partial_{3}f\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{(\partial_{3}\varphi)^{2}}\right)\partial_{t}\partial_{3}\varphi.

Note that
\partial_{3}\varphi|_{\Sigma}=1
, (
7.15
) then yields

\displaystyle\partial_{t}^{2}\overline{\partial}^{\varphi}_{j}\check{q}=\overline{\partial}^{\varphi}_{j}\mathcal{Q}+\mathbf{C}_{j}(\check{q}),\quad\text{ on }\Sigma,

(7.17)

where
\mathbf{C}_{i}(\check{q})=\overline{\partial}^{\varphi}_{i}\partial_{3}\check{q}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi-[\partial_{t}^{2},\partial_{i}\psi,\partial_{3}\check{q}]
when
i=1,2
, and
\mathbf{C}_{3}(\check{q})=\partial_{3}^{2}\check{q}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi
.
Therefore, the equation (
7.13
) turns into

\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi=-\frac{1}{\rho}N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}-\frac{1}{\rho}N\cdot\mathbf{C}(\check{q})+\mathbf{R}_{\psi}+\mathbf{R}_{\rho}+\mathbf{R}_{\psi,\rho},\quad\text{ on }\Sigma.

(7.18)

Parallel to
\mathcal{Q}
, we define
\mathcal{V}
to be the Alinhac’s good unknown of
v
associated with
\partial_{t}^{2}
:

\mathcal{V}:=\partial_{t}^{2}v-\partial_{t}^{2}\varphi\partial^{\varphi}_{3}v,\quad\text{ in }\Omega.

(7.19)

Then, similar to (
4.43
)–(
4.44
),
(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{Q})
verifies

\displaystyle\rho D_{t}^{\varphi}\mathcal{V}+\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}=G^{1},\quad

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega,

(7.20)

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}D_{t}^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}+\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot\mathcal{V}=G^{2}-C_{i}(v^{i}),\quad

\displaystyle\text{ in }\Omega,

where we write
\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(q)=\lambda^{2}
for simplicity of notations (which is reasonable when discussing the incompressible limit according to the discussion in Section
1.3
)

\displaystyle G^{1}_{i}=-[\partial_{t}^{2},\rho]D_{t}^{\varphi}v_{i}-\rho D(v_{i})-C_{i}(\check{q})-(\partial_{t}^{2}\rho)g\delta_{i3},\quad i=1,2,3

\displaystyle G^{2}=-\lambda^{2}D(\check{q})+\lambda^{2}g\partial_{t}^{2}v_{3}.

Here, for a generic function
f
, we define

D(f)=(D_{t}^{\varphi}\partial^{\varphi}_{3}f)(\partial_{t}^{2}\varphi)+D^{\prime}(f),

with

\displaystyle D^{\prime}(f)=[\partial_{t}^{2},\overline{v}]\cdot\overline{\partial}{}f+\left[\partial_{t}^{2},\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi),\partial_{3}f\right]+\left[\partial_{t}^{2},v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi,\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}\right]\partial_{3}f

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}[\partial_{t}^{2},v]\cdot\mathbf{N}\partial_{3}f-(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi)\partial_{3}f\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{(\partial_{3}\varphi)^{2}}\right)\partial_{t}\partial_{3}\varphi.

In the next, we commute the divergence operator
\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot
to the first equation of (
7.20
) to obtain:

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \rho\lambda^{2}(D_{t}^{\varphi})^{2}\mathcal{Q}-\Delta^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}=\rho\partial^{\varphi}_{i}v^{k}\partial^{\varphi}_{k}\mathcal{V}^{i}+\nabla^{\varphi}\rho\cdot D_{t}^{\varphi}\mathcal{V}+\rho D_{t}^{\varphi}\left(G^{2}-C_{i}(v^{i})\right)-\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot G^{1}.\end{aligned}

(7.21)

7.1.3
Decomposition of the pressure: Dirichlet-to-Neumann operator

Since

\mathcal{Q}=\partial_{t}^{2}\check{q}-\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\partial_{3}\check{q}=\sigma\partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{H}-\partial_{3}q\partial_{t}^{2}\psi,\quad\text{ on }\Sigma,

we define
\mathcal{Q}=\mathcal{Q}_{h}+\mathcal{Q}_{w}
, where
\mathcal{Q}_{h}
solves the elliptic equation

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -\Delta^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{h}=0,\quad&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\mathcal{Q}_{h}=\sigma\partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{H}-\partial_{3}q\partial_{t}^{2}\psi,\quad&\text{ on }\Sigma,\\
\partial_{3}\mathcal{Q}_{h}=0,\quad&\text{ on }\Sigma_{b},\end{aligned}

(7.22)

and
\mathcal{Q}_{w}
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} -\Delta^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}=-\rho\lambda^{2}(D_{t}^{\varphi})^{2}\mathcal{Q}+\rho\partial^{\varphi}_{i}v^{k}\partial^{\varphi}_{k}\mathcal{V}^{i}+\nabla^{\varphi}\rho\cdot D_{t}^{\varphi}\mathcal{V}+\rho D_{t}^{\varphi}\left(G^{2}-C_{i}(v^{i})\right)-\nabla^{\varphi}\cdot G^{1},\quad&\text{ in }\Omega,\\
\mathcal{Q}_{w}=0,\quad&\text{ on }\Sigma,\\
\partial_{3}\mathcal{Q}_{w}=\partial_{3}\mathcal{Q}=-\partial_{t}^{2}\rho g,\quad&\text{ on }\Sigma_{b},\end{aligned}

(7.23)

where
\partial_{3}\mathcal{Q}|_{\Sigma}
is computed by restricting the third component of the first equation in (
7.20
) on
\Sigma_{b}
.

With this decomposition, we can further reduce the evolution equation of the free surface (
7.18
) by introducing the Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) operator.

Definition 7.1

(Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) operator)
.

For a function
f:\Sigma\to\mathbb{R}
, we define the Dirichlet-to-Neumann (DtN) operator associated with
(\Omega,\psi)
by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}f:=N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}(\mathcal{E}_{\psi}f),

(7.24)

where
\mathcal{E}_{\psi}f
is defined to be the harmonic extension of
f
into
\Omega
, namely

\displaystyle-\Delta^{\varphi}(\mathcal{E}_{\psi}f)=0\quad\text{ in }\Omega,\quad\mathcal{E}_{\psi}f=f\quad\text{ on }\Sigma,\quad\partial_{3}(\mathcal{E}_{\psi}f)=0\quad\text{ on }\Sigma_{b}.

(7.25)

With this definition, we can rewrite

\displaystyle N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}=

\displaystyle~N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{h}+N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}=\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}(\sigma\partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{H}-\partial_{3}q\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)+N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sigma\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}(\partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{H})-\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}(\partial_{3}q\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)+N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w},

and thus the evolution equation (
7.18
) becomes

\displaystyle\rho\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi+\sigma\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}(\partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{H})-\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}(\partial_{3}q\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)=-N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}-N\cdot\mathbf{C}(\check{q})+\rho(\mathbf{R}_{\psi}+\mathbf{R}_{\rho}+\mathbf{R}_{\psi,\rho})\quad\text{ on }\Sigma

(7.26)

7.2
Preliminaries on pardifferential calculus

In the equation (
7.26
), the term involving DtN operators are fully nonlinear, so we shall find out their concrete forms in order for an explicit energy estimate. In the remaining part of this paper, we will introduce several preliminary lemmas about paradifferential calculus that have been proven in Alazard-Burq-Zuily
[
2
]
. Following the notations in Métivier
[
52
]
, we first introduce the basic definition of a paradifferential operator. Note that the dimension
d
in this section is actually the Hausdorff dimension of the free surface.

Definition 7.2

(Symbols)
.

Given
r\geq 0,~m\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote
\Gamma_{r}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to be the space of locally bounded functions
a(x^{\prime},\xi)
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\})
, which are
C^{\infty}
with respect to
\xi(\xi\neq\mathbf{0})
, such that for any
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{d},\xi\neq\mathbf{0}
, the function
x^{\prime}\mapsto\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}a(x^{\prime},\xi)
belongs to
W^{r,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and there exists a constant
C_{\alpha}
such that

\left|\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}a(\cdot,\xi)\right|_{W^{r,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq C_{\alpha}(1+|\xi|)^{m-|\alpha|},~~\forall|\xi|\geq 1/2.

Definition 7.3

(Paradifferential operator)
.

Given a symbol
a
, we shall define the
paradifferential operator
T_{a}
by

\displaystyle\widehat{T_{a}u}(\xi):=(2\pi)^{-d}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\tilde{\chi}(\xi-\eta,\eta)\hat{a}(\xi-\eta,\eta)\phi(\eta)\hat{u}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta

(7.27)

where
\hat{a}(\theta,\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\exp(-ix^{\prime}\cdot\theta)a(x^{\prime},\xi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}
is the Fourier transform of
a
in variable
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Here
\tilde{\chi}
and
\phi
are two given cut-off functions such that

\phi(\eta)=0~~\text{for }|\eta|\leq 1,\quad\phi(\eta)=1~~\text{for }|\eta|\geq 2,

and
\tilde{\chi}(\theta,\eta)
is homogeneous of degree 0 and satisfies that for
0<\varepsilon_{1}<\varepsilon_{2}\ll 1
,
\tilde{\chi}(\theta,\eta)=1
if
|\theta|\leq\varepsilon_{1}|\eta|
and
\tilde{\chi}(\theta,\eta)=0
if
|\theta|\geq\varepsilon_{2}|\eta|
. We also introduce the semi-norm

\displaystyle M_{r}^{a}(a):=\sup_{|\alpha|\leq\frac{d}{2}+1+r}\sup_{|\xi|\geq 1/2}\left|(1+|\xi|)^{|\alpha|-m}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}a(\cdot,\xi)\right|_{W^{r,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(7.28)

For
m\in\mathbb{R}
, we say
T
is of order
m
if for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
T
is bounded from
H^{s}
to
H^{s-m}
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
. If
a\in\Gamma_{0}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
T_{a}
is of order
m
. Moreover, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a constant
K
such that
\|T_{a}\|_{H^{s}\to H^{s-m}}\leq KM_{0}^{m}(a)
.

Proposition 7.3

(Composition,
[
2
, Theorem 3.7]
)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
and
r>0
. If
a\in\Gamma_{r}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),~b\in\Gamma_{r}^{m^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then
T_{a}T_{b}-T_{a\#b}
is of order
m+m^{\prime}-r
where

a\#b:=\sum_{|\alpha|<r}\frac{1}{i^{|\alpha|}\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}a\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{\alpha}b

and
\partial_{x^{\prime}}=(\overline{\partial}{}_{x_{1}},\overline{\partial}{}_{x_{2}})
. Moreover, for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a constant
K
such that

\displaystyle\|T_{a}T_{b}-T_{a\#b}\|_{H^{s}\to H^{s-m-m^{\prime}+r}}\leq KM_{r}^{m}(a)M_{r}^{m^{\prime}}(b).

(7.29)

Proposition 7.4

(Adjoint,
[
2
, Theorem 3.10]
)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R}
,
r>0
and
a\in\Gamma_{r}^{m}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We denote by
(T_{a})^{*}
the adjoint operator of
T_{a}
. Then
(T_{a})^{*}-T_{a^{*}}
is of order
m-r
where

a^{*}:=\sum_{|\alpha|<r}\frac{1}{i^{\alpha}\alpha!}\partial_{\xi}^{\alpha}\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{\alpha}\bar{a}.

Moreover, for any
s\in\mathbb{R}
, there exists a constant
K
such that
\|(T_{a})^{*}-T_{a^{*}}\|_{H^{s}\to H^{s-m+r}}\leq KM_{r}^{m}(a)
.

Here and thereafter in this section,
\psi\in C([0,T];H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
is a given function with
s>2+\frac{d}{2}
. The symbolic calculus is not defined for
C^{\infty}
symbols, so we need to introduce the following symbols.

Definition 7.4
.

Given
m\in\mathbb{R}
, we denote
\Sigma^{m}
to be the class of symbols
a
of the form
a=a^{(m)}+a^{(m-1)}
with

a^{(m)}(t,x^{\prime},\xi)=F(\partial_{x^{\prime}}\psi(t,x^{\prime}),\xi),\quad a^{(m-1)}(t,x^{\prime},\xi)=\sum_{|\alpha|_{2}}G_{\alpha}(\partial_{x^{\prime}}\psi(t,x^{\prime}),\xi)\partial_{x^{\prime}}^{\alpha}\psi(t,x^{\prime})

such that

i.

T_{a}
maps real-valued functions to real-valued functions;

ii.

F
is a
C^{\infty}
real-valued functions of
(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\})
, homogeneous of degree
m
in
\xi
, such that there exists a continuous function
K=K(\zeta)>0
such that
F(\zeta,\xi)\geq K(\zeta)|\xi|^{m}
for all
(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\})
;

iii.

G_{\alpha}
is a
C^{\infty}
complex-valued function of
(\zeta,\xi)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\{0\})
, homogeneous of degree
m-1
in
\xi
.

Definition 7.5

(Equivalence of paradifferential operators)
.

Given
m\in\mathbb{R}
and consider two families of operators of order
m
:
\{A(t):t\in[0,T]\}
and
\{B(t):t\in[0,T]\}
, we say
A\sim B
if
A-B
has order
m-1.5
and satisfies the estimate: for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
there exists a continuous function
C(\cdot)
such that

\forall t\in[0,T],~~\|A(t)-B(t)\|_{H^{r}\to H^{r-(m-1.5)}}\leq C(|\psi(t)|_{s+\frac{1}{2}}).

From now on, we use the notation
|\cdot|_{s_{1}\to s_{2}}
to represent the operator norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s_{1}}\to H^{s_{2}}}
, and use the notation
|\cdot|_{s}
to represent
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. We have the following theorem for the composition

Proposition 7.5

(
[
2
, Prop. 4.3]
)
.

Let
m,m^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

1.

If
a\in\Sigma^{m},~b\in\Sigma^{m^{\prime}}
, then
T_{a}T_{b}\sim T_{a\#b}
where
a\#b
is given by

a\#b=a^{(m)}b^{(m^{\prime})}+a^{(m-1)}b^{(m^{\prime})}+a^{(m)}b^{(m^{\prime}-1)}+\frac{1}{i}\partial_{\xi}a^{(m)}\cdot\partial_{x^{\prime}}b^{(m^{\prime})}.

2.

If
a\in\Sigma^{m}
, then
(T_{a})^{*}\sim T_{b}
where
b\in\Sigma^{m}
is given by

b=a^{(m)}+\overline{a^{(m-1)}}+\frac{1}{i}(\partial_{x^{\prime}}\cdot\partial_{\xi})a^{(m)}.

We denote
\Re z
and
\Im z
to be the real part and the imaginary part of a complex number
z
, respectively. As a corollary, we have

Corollary 7.6

(
[
2
, Prop. 4.3(2)]
)
.

If
a\in\Sigma^{m}
satisfies
\Im a^{(m-1)}=-\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{\xi}\cdot\partial_{x^{\prime}})a^{(m)}
, then
(T_{a})^{*}\sim T_{a}
.

The next proposition is significant for estimates in Sobolev norms via paradifferential calculus.

Proposition 7.7

(
[
2
, Prop. 4.4 and 4.6]
)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{R},~r\in\mathbb{R}
. Then for all symbol
a\in\Sigma^{m}
and
t\in[0,T]
, the following estimate holds.

\displaystyle|T_{a(t)}u|_{r-m}\leq

\displaystyle~C(|\psi(t)|_{s-1})|u|_{r},

(7.30)

\displaystyle|u|_{r+m}\leq

\displaystyle~C(|\psi(t)|_{s-1})\left(|T_{a(t)}u|_{r}+|u|_{0}\right).

(7.31)

7.3
Paralinearization of evolution equation of the free surface

Now we can start to paralinearize the term involving
\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}
and
\mathcal{H}
in (
7.26
).

Lemma 7.8

(Paralinearization of the DtN operator,
[
5
, Sect. 4.4]
)
.

For
f,\psi\in H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}f=T_{\Lambda}f+R_{\Lambda}^{\psi}(f),

(7.32)

with the symbols
\Lambda=\Lambda^{(1)}+\Lambda^{(0)}
give by

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(1)}=

\displaystyle\sqrt{(1+|\overline{\nabla}_{x^{\prime}}\psi|^{2})|\xi|^{2}-(\overline{\nabla}_{x^{\prime}}\psi\cdot\xi)^{2}},

(7.33)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{(0)}=

\displaystyle\frac{1+|\overline{\nabla}_{x^{\prime}}\psi|^{2}|}{2\Lambda^{(1)}}\left(\overline{\nabla}_{x^{\prime}}\cdot(\alpha^{(1)}\overline{\nabla}_{x^{\prime}}\psi)+i\partial_{\xi}\Lambda^{(1)}\cdot\partial_{x^{\prime}}\alpha^{(1)}\right),

(7.34)

and
\alpha^{(1)}:=(\Lambda^{(1)}+i\overline{\nabla}_{x^{\prime}}\psi\cdot\xi)/(1+|\overline{\nabla}_{x^{\prime}}\psi|^{2})
. The remainder terms satisfy the following estimates

\displaystyle|R_{\Lambda}^{\psi}(f)|_{r}\leq C(|\psi|_{s+\frac{1}{2}})|f|_{r}\quad\forall\frac{1}{2}\leq r\leq s-\frac{1}{2},~s>2+\frac{d}{2}.

(7.35)

Next, we paralinearize the mean curvature term. Let
\mathcal{H}(\psi)=-\overline{\nabla}\cdot\left(\frac{\overline{\nabla}\psi}{\sqrt{1+|\overline{\nabla}\psi|^{2}}}\right)
. We have

Lemma 7.9

(Paralinearization of the mean curvature,
[
2
, Lemma 3.25]
)
.

There holds
\mathcal{H}(\psi)=T_{\mathfrak{H}}\psi+R_{\mathfrak{H}}
where
\mathfrak{H}=\mathfrak{H}^{(2)}+\mathfrak{H}^{(1)}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}^{(2)}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\overline{\nabla}_{x^{\prime}}\psi|^{2}}}\left(|\xi|^{2}-\frac{(\overline{\nabla}_{x^{\prime}}\psi\cdot\xi)^{2}}{1+|\overline{\nabla}_{x^{\prime}}\psi|^{2}}\right),

(7.36)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle-\frac{i}{2}(\partial_{x^{\prime}}\cdot\partial_{\xi})\mathfrak{H}^{(2)},

(7.37)

and the remainder term
R_{\mathfrak{H}}
satisfies

\displaystyle|R_{\mathfrak{H}}|_{2s-3}\leq C(|\psi|_{s+\frac{1}{2}}).

(7.38)

Now, we can treat the nonlinear terms on the left side of (
7.26
). The term involving surface tension is treated as follows

\displaystyle\sigma\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}(\partial_{t}^{2}\mathcal{H}(\psi))=

\displaystyle~\sigma\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}(\partial_{t}^{2}T_{\mathfrak{H}}\psi)+\sigma\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}(\partial_{t}^{2}R_{\mathfrak{H}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\sigma T_{\Lambda}T_{\mathfrak{H}}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)+\sigma\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}([\partial_{t}^{2},T_{\mathfrak{H}}]\psi+\partial_{t}^{2}R_{\mathfrak{H}})+\sigma R_{\Lambda}^{\psi}(T_{\mathfrak{H}}\partial_{t}^{2}(\psi))

(7.39)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle~\sigma T_{\Lambda}T_{\mathfrak{H}}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)+\mathbf{R}^{ST}_{\psi}

The term involving the Rayleigh-Taylor sign is treated as follows

\displaystyle\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}(\partial_{3}q\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)=

\displaystyle~(\partial_{3}q)\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)+[\mathfrak{N}_{\psi},\partial_{3}q]\partial_{t}^{2}\psi.

(7.40)

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle~(\partial_{3}q)\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)+\mathbf{R}^{RT}_{\psi}

Now, the evolution equation (
7.26
) becomes

\displaystyle\rho\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi+\sigma T_{\Lambda}T_{\mathfrak{H}}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)+(-\partial_{3}q)\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)=

\displaystyle-N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}+\mathbf{R}^{\sigma}_{\psi}+\mathbf{R}^{RT}_{\psi}

(7.41)

\displaystyle-N\cdot\mathbf{C}(\check{q})+\rho(\mathbf{R}_{\psi}+\mathbf{R}_{\rho}+\mathbf{R}_{\psi,\rho})\quad\text{ on }\Sigma.

7.4
Uniform estimates for the free surface

In order for an explicit energy estimate via (
7.41
), we shall symmetrize the 3-rd order paradifferential operator
T_{\Lambda}T_{\mathfrak{H}}
. That is, find suitable symbols
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\Sigma^{1.5}
and
{\mathfrak{n}}\in\Sigma^{0}
such that
T_{\mathfrak{n}}T_{\Lambda}T_{\mathfrak{H}}\sim T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
T_{\mathfrak{m}}\sim(T_{\mathfrak{m}})^{*}
.

Proposition 7.10

(Symmetrization,
[
2
, Prop. 4.8]
)
.

Let
{\mathfrak{n}}\in\Sigma^{0}
and
{\mathfrak{m}}\in\Sigma^{1.5}
be defined by

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{n}}:=

\displaystyle~\frac{1}{\sqrt[4]{1+|\overline{\nabla}\psi|^{2}}}=|N|^{-\frac{1}{2}},

(7.42)

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{m}}:=

\displaystyle~\underbrace{\sqrt{\mathfrak{H}^{(2)}\Lambda^{(1)}}}_{=:{\mathfrak{m}}^{(1.5)}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2i}(\partial_{\xi}\cdot\partial_{x^{\prime}})\sqrt{\mathfrak{H}^{(2)}\Lambda^{(1)}}}_{=:{\mathfrak{m}}^{(0.5)}}.

(7.43)

Then
T_{\mathfrak{n}}T_{\lambda}T_{\mathfrak{H}}\sim T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}
and
T_{\mathfrak{m}}\sim(T_{\mathfrak{m}})^{*}
are both fulfilled.

Recall that we need the uniform bounds for
|\partial_{t}^{3}\psi|_{1.5}^{2}
, so we shall take 1.5-th order derivative in (
7.41
). Since the symbol
{\mathfrak{m}}
also belongs to
\Sigma^{1.5}
, we alternatively consider the
T_{\mathfrak{m}}
-differentiated evolution equation thanks to the symmetrization result. We introduce the following energy functional

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{M}}(t):=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\rho\left|T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|^{2}+\sigma\left|T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|^{2}+\frac{c_{0}}{2}\left|\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}.

(7.44)

In view of Proposition
7.7
and Lemma
D.2
, we have the comparison between
{\mathfrak{M}}(t)
and standard Sobolev norms

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{M}}(t)\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|_{1.5}^{2}+\sigma|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{3}^{2}+\frac{c_{0}}{4}|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{2}^{2};

(7.45)

\displaystyle\left|\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|_{1.5}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|_{0}^{2}+\left|\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|_{0}^{2},

(7.46)

\displaystyle\sigma|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{3}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle~\sigma\left|T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|^{2}+\sigma|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{0}^{2},\quad|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{2}^{2}\lesssim\left|\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|_{0}^{2}+|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{0}^{2}.

(7.47)

For those
L^{2}(\Sigma)
norms, we invoke the kinematic boundary condition, trace lemma and Young’s inequality to get

|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{0}^{2}\lesssim\|v_{t}\|_{1}^{2}|\overline{\nabla}\psi|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|v\|_{1}^{2}|\overline{\nabla}\partial_{t}\psi|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}

and

\displaystyle|\partial_{t}^{3}\psi|_{0}^{2}\lesssim\varepsilon\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{0}^{2}|\overline{\nabla}\psi|_{L^{\infty}}^{4}+\|v_{t}\|_{1}^{2}|\overline{\nabla}\partial_{t}\psi|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|v\|_{2}^{2}|\overline{\nabla}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{0}^{2}.

The
\varepsilon
-term contributes to
\varepsilon\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
. The term
\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{0}^{2}
can be controlled via
\partial_{t}^{2}
-estimates of (
1.18
) in which there is no loss of
\lambda
-weight in the corresponding commutators of Alinhac good unknowns, as we take a full-time derivative
\partial_{t}^{2}
. The other terms contain at most one time derivatives and thus can be controlled directly. Thus, we have

\displaystyle{\mathfrak{M}}(t)\lesssim\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)\text{ and }\left|\partial_{t}^{3}\psi\right|_{1.5}^{2}+\sigma|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{3}^{2}+|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{2}^{2}\lesssim{\mathfrak{M}}(t)+\varepsilon\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)+\text{controllable terms}.

(7.48)

So, it suffices to control
{\mathfrak{M}}(t)
in order to establish the bound for
\left|\partial_{t}^{3}\psi\right|_{1.5}^{2}+\sigma|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{3}^{2}+|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{2}^{2}
in
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
.

Now we start to control
{\mathfrak{M}}(t)
. Taking the partial derivative in the first term, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}\rho\left|T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

(7.49)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}(\rho\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}+\int_{\Sigma}[\rho-1,T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}]\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\,(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}(\partial_{t}\rho+\overline{\nabla}\cdot(\rho\overline{v}))\left|T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}+\int_{\Sigma}\rho[\overline{D_{t}},T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}](\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}(\rho\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}+R_{1}^{M}

Next, plugging the paralinearized equation (
7.41
) into the above equality, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Sigma}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}(\rho\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)(T_{\mathfrak{m}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

(7.50)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sigma\int_{\Sigma}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}T_{\Lambda}T_{\mathfrak{H}}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\int_{\Sigma}(-\partial_{3}q)T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega}[T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}},\partial_{3}q]\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L}}{{=}}

\displaystyle-\sigma\int_{\Sigma}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,T_{\mathfrak{m}}(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\int_{\Sigma}(-\partial_{3}q)\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}(N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w})\,(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}+\int_{\Omega}[T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}},\partial_{3}q]\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega}[T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}},\partial_{3}q]\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\int_{\Omega}(-\partial_{3}q)[T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}},\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}]\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\,(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,[\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}},\partial_{3}q](T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle+\int_{\Sigma}(\mathbf{R}^{ST}_{\psi}+\mathbf{R}^{RT}_{\psi}-N\cdot\mathbf{C}(\check{q})+\rho(\mathbf{R}_{\psi}+\mathbf{R}_{\rho}+\mathbf{R}_{\psi,\rho}))\,(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle M^{ST}+M^{RT}+M^{W}+R_{2}^{M}+R_{3}^{M}+R_{4}^{M}+R_{5}^{M}+R_{6}^{M}.

Here we use
T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\sim T_{\mathfrak{n}}T_{\Lambda}T_{\mathfrak{H}}
and
T_{\mathfrak{m}}\sim T_{\mathfrak{m}}^{*}
to derive
M^{ST}
and omit the low-order error terms in this equivalence. We also use the self-adjointness of DtN operator in
L^{2}(\Sigma)
to derive
M^{RT}
. Note that we may not use
T_{\Lambda}
to replace
\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}
in the term involving the Rayleigh-Taylor sign, as we do not have
T_{\Lambda}\sim T_{\Lambda}^{*}
. The major terms are
M^{ST},M^{RT}
and
M^{W}
, among which the first two terms contribute to the boundary regularity with or without
\sigma
-weight, while the term
M^{W}
contributes to a fifth-order term that motivates us to involve
E_{5}(t)
in the energy functional
\mathfrak{E}(t)
. The control of
R_{2}^{M}\sim R_{6}^{M}
and other commutators generated by
M^{ST},M^{RT}
and
M^{W}
will be postponed at the end of this section.

The term
M^{ST}
contributes to
\sqrt{\sigma}
-weighted boundary regularity. We have

\displaystyle M^{ST}=

\displaystyle-\sigma\int_{\Sigma}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,T_{\mathfrak{m}}(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

(7.51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}\left|T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\sigma\int_{\Sigma}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}(\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,[T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}},\overline{D_{t}}]\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\frac{\sigma}{2}(\overline{\nabla}\cdot\overline{v})\left|T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}\left|T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}+R_{7}^{M}.

The term
M^{RT}
contributes to non-weighted boundary regularity with the help of Rayleigh-Taylor sign condition
-\partial_{3}q\geq c_{0}/2>0
. We have

\displaystyle M^{RT}=

\displaystyle-\int_{\Sigma}(-\partial_{3}q)\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

(7.52)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}(-\partial_{3}q)\left|\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle+\int_{\Sigma}\partial_{3}q\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,[\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}},\overline{D_{t}}]\partial_{t}^{2}\psi\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}\partial_{3}q+\overline{\nabla}\cdot(\partial_{3}q\overline{v})\left|\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=:

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\int_{\Omega}(-\partial_{3}q)\left|\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}}(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\right|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}+R_{8}^{M}.

Currently, we have arrived at the following energy inequality

{\mathfrak{M}}(t)\leq{\mathfrak{M}}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}M^{W}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau+\sum_{j=1}^{8}\int_{0}^{t}R_{j}^{M}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

(7.53)

7.5
Weighted fifth-order energy

7.5.1
Necessity of fifth-order energy

Recall that
M^{W}=-\int_{\Sigma}T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}(N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w})\,(T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi)\,\mathrm{d}x^{\prime}
and
T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}
is a 1.5-th order paradifferential operator, so it remains to control
|N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}|_{1.5}
in order for the control of
M^{W}
. Using trace theorem, we have
|N\cdot\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}|_{1.5}\leq|\overline{\nabla}\psi|_{1.5}\|\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}\|_{2}
. Then, we use the following div-curl inequality

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}\|_{2}^{2}\leq C(|\overline{\nabla}\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}},|\psi|_{2.5})\left(\|\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}\|_{0}^{2}+\|\Delta^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}\|_{1}^{2}+\|\nabla^{\varphi}\times\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}\|_{1}^{2}+|N\times\nabla^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}|_{1.5}^{2}+|\partial_{3}\mathcal{Q}_{w}|_{H^{1.5}(\Sigma_{b})}^{2}\right),

(7.54)

where the third and the fourth terms are all zero because
\nabla^{\varphi}\times\nabla^{\varphi}f=\bf{0}
and
\mathcal{Q}_{w}
has zero boundary value on
\Sigma
. The fifth term is easy to control, we have

\displaystyle|\partial_{3}\mathcal{Q}_{w}|_{H^{1.5}(\Sigma_{b})}^{2}=|\partial_{t}^{2}\rho g|_{H^{1.5}(\Sigma_{b})}^{2}\lesssim\lambda^{2}\|\partial_{t}^{2}q\|_{2}^{2}.

(7.55)

The first term is of lower-order and we omit the treatment. For the second term, invoking (
7.23
), we have

\displaystyle\|\Delta^{\varphi}\mathcal{Q}_{w}\|_{1}\lesssim

\displaystyle~C\left(\sum_{k=2}^{4}|\lambda^{(k-2)_{+}}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi|_{4-k}\right)\left(\sum_{k=2}^{4}\|\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{k}\check{q}\|_{5-k}+\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2}\|\partial v\|_{2}+\lambda^{2}\|\partial q\|_{2}\|\partial_{t}^{3}v\|_{1}+P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t))\right),

(7.56)

where the first term requires the control of
E_{5}(t)
. It should be noted that the
\lambda^{2}
in the third term is generated from
\nabla^{\varphi}\rho
such that the term
D_{t}^{\varphi}\mathcal{V}
can be controlled without loss of
\lambda
-weight. The last two term on the right side of (
7.23
) can be directly controlled by
P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t))
, as the number of derivatives does not exceed 4 and the number of time derivatives does not exceed 2. Therefore, the energy inequality (
7.57
) becomes

{\mathfrak{M}}(t)\leq{\mathfrak{M}}(0)+\int_{0}^{t}P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(\tau))E_{5}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau+\sum_{j=1}^{8}\int_{0}^{t}R_{j}^{M}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau,

(7.57)

and it remains to control

E_{5}(t):=\sum_{k=0}^{5}\left\|\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{k}(v,\lambda^{(k-4)_{+}}\check{q})\right\|_{5-k}^{2}+\left|\sqrt{\sigma}\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi\right|_{6-k}^{2}+\left|\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{k}\psi\right|_{5-k}^{2}

uniformly in
{\lambda,\sigma}
.

7.5.2
Control of
E_{5}(t)
and the remaining terms in
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)

Notice that
E_{5}(t)
has exactly the same structure as the energy
E(t)
used to prove the local existence in Theorem
1.1
if we remove the weight
\lambda^{2}
: all quantities except the top-order time derivative of
\check{q}
share the same weight of Mach number. This indicates us to use the div-curl inequality in Lemma
4.2
and follow the same strategies as in Section
4
instead of using the one in Lemma
7.1
to establish the following energy inequality

\displaystyle E_{5}(t)\leq P(\mathfrak{E}(0))+P(\mathfrak{E}(t))\int_{0}^{t}P(\mathfrak{E}(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

(7.58)

Also, notice that
\partial_{t}^{3}v,\partial_{t}^{3}\check{q}
and
\partial_{t}^{4}v,\partial_{t}^{4}\check{q}
in
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
also share the same weight of Mach number, so we can still control them by following the same strategies as in Section
4
. What’s different is that the high-order time derivatives in both
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
and
E_{5}(t)
may need more weights in order for the uniform boundedness. Thus, it remains to carefully check if there is any loss of
\lambda
-weight in the control of commutators
\mathfrak{C}^{\prime}(\check{q}),\mathfrak{C}_{i}^{\prime}(v_{i})
and
\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}(\check{q}),\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}(v_{i})
in the tangential estimates. Let us recall the concrete forms of these commutators when
\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}
has the form
\overline{\partial}{}^{k}\partial_{t}^{l}
.

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{i}^{\prime}(f)=\left[\mathcal{T}^{\alpha},\frac{\mathbf{N}_{i}}{\partial_{3}\varphi},\partial_{3}f\right]+\partial_{3}f\left[\mathcal{T}^{\alpha},\mathbf{N}_{i},\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}\right]+\mathbf{N}_{i}\partial_{3}f\left[\mathcal{T}^{\alpha-\gamma},\frac{1}{(\partial_{3}\varphi)^{2}}\right]\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}\partial_{3}\varphi

(7.59)

with
|\gamma|=1
, and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}^{\prime}(f)=

\displaystyle~[\mathcal{T}^{\alpha},\overline{v}]\cdot\overline{\partial}{}f+\left[\mathcal{T}^{\alpha},\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi),\partial_{3}f\right]+\left[\mathcal{T}^{\alpha},v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi,\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}\right]\partial_{3}f+\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}[\mathcal{T}^{\alpha},v]\cdot\mathbf{N}\partial_{3}f

\displaystyle-(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi)\partial_{3}f\left[\mathcal{T}^{\alpha-\gamma},\frac{1}{(\partial_{3}\varphi)^{2}}\right]\mathcal{T}^{\gamma}\partial_{3}\varphi.

(7.60)

Here, we only check the most difficult cases and omit the other easier ones:
\mathfrak{C}(\check{q})
and
\mathfrak{D}(\check{q})
in
\lambda\partial_{t}^{3}\overline{\partial}{}
-estimates,
\lambda\partial_{t}^{4}
-estimates (for
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
),
\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{4}\overline{\partial}{}
-estimates and
\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{5}
-estimates (for
E_{5}(t)
). Note that there is no need to check the same commutators for
v_{i}
because the power of
\lambda
weight that
\partial_{t}^{k}v_{i}
needs never exceeds that for
\partial_{t}^{k}\check{q}
.

\lambda\partial_{t}^{3}\overline{\partial}{}
-estimates for
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
.

We shall control
\|\lambda\mathfrak{C}_{i}(\check{q})\|_{0}
uniformly in
\lambda
when
\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}=\partial_{t}^{3}\overline{\partial}{}
and
f=\check{q}
. The worst case is that all time derivatives fall on
\check{q}
and such terms have the following forms

\lambda\overline{\partial}{}(\mathbf{N}_{i}/\partial_{3}\varphi)\partial_{t}^{3}\partial_{3}\check{q},\quad\lambda\overline{\partial}{}\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi)\right)\partial_{t}^{3}\partial_{3}\check{q}

whose
L^{2}
norms are bounded by
P(|\overline{\nabla}\psi,\partial_{t}\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}},\|\overline{\partial}{}v\|_{L^{\infty}})\|\lambda\partial_{t}^{3}\check{q}\|_{1}
.

\lambda\partial_{t}^{4}
-estimates for
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
.

We shall control
\|\lambda\mathfrak{C}_{i}(\check{q})\|_{0}
uniformly in
\lambda
when
\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}=\partial_{t}^{4}
and
f=\check{q}
. The worst case is that three out of the four time derivatives fall on
\check{q}
and such terms have the following forms

\lambda\partial_{t}(\mathbf{N}_{i}/\partial_{3}\varphi)\partial_{t}^{3}\partial_{3}\check{q},\quad\lambda\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi)\right)\partial_{t}^{3}\partial_{3}\check{q}

whose
L^{2}
norms are bounded by
P(|\overline{\nabla}\psi,\partial_{t}\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}},\|\overline{\partial}{}v\|_{L^{\infty}},|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{L^{\infty}})\|\lambda\partial_{t}^{3}\check{q}\|_{1}
.

\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{4}\overline{\partial}{}
-estimates for
E_{5}(t)
.

We shall control
\|\lambda^{2}\mathfrak{C}_{i}(\check{q})\|_{0}
uniformly in
\lambda
when
\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}=\partial_{t}^{4}\overline{\partial}{}
and
f=\check{q}
. Although every quantity in
E_{5}(t)
needs
\lambda^{2}
-weight, the terms involving
\check{q}
becomes a lower order term and contains at most one time derivative if there are 5 derivatives falling on
\mathbf{N}
or
v\cdot\mathbf{N}
. Since
\partial_{t}\nabla\check{q}
is uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}(\Omega)
, there is no need to out extra effort on such terms. The worst case is still that all time derivatives fall on
\check{q}
and such terms have the following forms

\lambda^{2}\overline{\partial}{}(\mathbf{N}_{i}/\partial_{3}\varphi)\partial_{t}^{4}\partial_{3}\check{q},\quad\lambda^{2}\overline{\partial}{}\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi)\right)\partial_{t}^{4}\partial_{3}\check{q}

whose
L^{2}
norms are bounded by
P(|\overline{\nabla}\psi,\partial_{t}\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}},\|\overline{\partial}{}v\|_{L^{\infty}})\|\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{4}\check{q}\|_{1}
. As for the intermediate terms, we check the case that
\partial_{t}^{3}
falls on
\partial_{3}\check{q}
and
\overline{\partial}{}\partial_{t}
falls on
(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi)
because neither of these two terms can be uniformly bounded in
L^{\infty}
. We have that

\displaystyle\left\|\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}{}((\partial_{3}\varphi)^{-1}(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi))\partial_{t}^{3}\partial_{3}\check{q}\right\|_{0}\lesssim\|\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{3}\partial_{3}\check{q}\|_{L^{6}}\|\partial_{t}\overline{\partial}{}((\partial_{3}\varphi)^{-1}(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi))\|_{L^{3}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{3}\check{q}\|_{2}(|\psi_{t}|_{2.5}+|\psi_{tt}|_{1.5})\|v_{t}\|_{3}P(|\overline{\nabla}\psi,\partial_{t}\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}})\leq\sqrt{E_{5}(t)}P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)).

\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{5}
-estimates for
E_{5}(t)
.

We shall control
\|\lambda^{2}\mathfrak{C}_{i}(\check{q})\|_{0}
uniformly in
\lambda
when
\mathcal{T}^{\alpha}=\partial_{t}^{5}
and
f=\check{q}
. Again, the worst case is still that all time derivatives fall on
\check{q}
and such terms have the following forms

\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}(\mathbf{N}_{i}/\partial_{3}\varphi)\partial_{t}^{4}\partial_{3}\check{q},\quad\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}\left(\frac{1}{\partial_{3}\varphi}(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi)\right)\partial_{t}^{4}\partial_{3}\check{q}

whose
L^{2}
norms are bounded by
P(|\overline{\nabla}\psi,\partial_{t}\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}},\|\overline{\partial}{}v\|_{L^{\infty}},|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{L^{\infty}})\|\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{4}\check{q}\|_{1}
. As for the intermediate terms, we check the case that
\partial_{t}^{3}
falls on
\partial_{3}\check{q}
and
\partial_{t}^{2}
falls on
(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi)
. We have that

\displaystyle\left\|\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}((\partial_{3}\varphi)^{-1}(v\cdot\mathbf{N}-\partial_{t}\varphi))\partial_{t}^{3}\partial_{3}\check{q}\right\|_{0}\lesssim\|\lambda\partial_{t}^{3}\check{q}\|_{0}(|\psi_{t}|_{2.5}+|\psi_{tt}|_{2.5}+|\lambda\psi_{ttt}|_{1.5})\|v_{t}\|_{3}P(|\overline{\nabla}\psi,\partial_{t}\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}})\leq P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)).

The omitted terms can be controlled in a similar or easier manner. Thus, we conclude that inequality (
7.58
) holds true.

7.5.3
Uniform estimates for
\mathfrak{E}(t)
and the incompressible limit

So far, we already obtain the following energy inequalities.

1.

The terms involving less than 2 time derivatives in
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{1}\|\partial_{t}^{k}v\|_{4-k}^{2}+\sigma|\partial_{t}^{k}\psi|_{5-k}^{2}+|\psi|_{4}^{2}+|\partial_{t}\psi|_{3.5}^{2}+\|\check{q}\|_{4}^{2}\lesssim P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(0))+\int_{0}^{t}P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau,

(7.61)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\check{q}\|_{3}^{2}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{2}v\|_{2}^{2}+P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(0))+\int_{0}^{t}P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

(7.62)

These two inequalities are proved in the same way as in Section
4
.

2.

The terms involving 3 and 4 time derivatives in
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k=3}^{4}\|\lambda\partial_{t}^{k}v,\lambda^{1+(k-3)_{+}}\partial_{t}^{k}\check{q}\|_{4-k}^{2}+\sigma|\lambda\partial_{t}^{k}\psi|_{5-k}^{2}+|\lambda\partial_{t}^{3}\psi|_{1.5}^{2}+|\lambda\partial_{t}^{4}\psi|_{0.5}^{2}\lesssim P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(0))+\int_{0}^{t}P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau,

(7.63)

which is obtained by following the same strategy as in Section
4
and the analysis of commutator in Section
7.5.2
.

3.

Control of
E_{5}(t)
:

\displaystyle E_{5}(t)\leq P(\mathfrak{E}(0))+P(\mathfrak{E}(t))\int_{0}^{t}P(\mathfrak{E}(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

(7.64)

4.

Control of
\|v_{tt}\|_{2}^{2}
in
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
via paradifferential calculus:

\displaystyle\|v_{tt}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle~\varepsilon\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{8}\int_{0}^{t}R_{j}^{M}(\tau)\,\mathrm{d}\tau+P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(0))+\int_{0}^{t}P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau,

(7.65)

\displaystyle\|\lambda\partial_{t}^{2}\check{q}\|_{2}^{2}\lesssim

\displaystyle~\|\lambda\partial_{t}^{3}v\|_{1}^{2}+P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(0))+\int_{0}^{t}P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau.

(7.66)

Summing up the above estimates, we can prove the Grönwall-type inequality for the energy functional
\mathfrak{E}(t)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}(t)\leq P(\mathfrak{E}(0))+P(\mathfrak{E}(t))\int_{0}^{t}P(\mathfrak{E}(\tau))\,\mathrm{d}\tau\quad\underline{\text{uniformly in }{\lambda,\sigma}},

(7.67)

provided that we have the bounds for the remainders
|R_{j}^{M}(t)|_{L^{2}}\leq P(\mathfrak{E}(t))
for
1\leq j\leq 8
. Since the first-order time derivatives in
\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)
still remain uniformly bounded, we can obtain the same convergence result as in Section
6.2
, and we no longer repeat the statement here.

7.6
Control of commutators involving paradifferential operators

At the end of this paper, it remains to prove that
|R_{j}^{M}(t)|_{L^{2}}\leq P(\mathfrak{E}(t))
for
1\leq j\leq 8
. It should be noted that there are many time derivatives involved in these remainders, so the commutator estimates shown in
[
2
]
may not be directly applied. In particular, we only show the control of most difficult ones:

•

Two commutators in
R_{1}^{M}
:
[T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}},\rho-1]\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi
and
[T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}},\overline{D_{t}}]\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi
.

•

The commutator in
R_{5}^{M}
:
[\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}},\partial_{3}q]T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi
.

•

The concrete forms of
\mathbf{R}_{\psi}^{ST}
and
\mathbf{R}_{\psi}^{RT}
.

The control of the other terms in these remainders will be omitted. In fact
R_{2}^{M},R_{3}^{M},R_{7}^{M},R_{8}^{M}
and can be controlled in the same way as
R_{1}^{M}
. The control of
R_{4}^{M}
is easier than that of
R_{3}^{M}
as the function contains less time derivatives. The other terms in
R_{6}^{M}
can be controlled in
L^{2}(\Sigma)
uniformly in
{\lambda,\sigma}
by directly counting the number of derivatives.

We start with the first one. Writing
f=\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi
and
a=\rho-1
for convenience, we have

\displaystyle[T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}},a]f=T_{\mathfrak{m}}([T_{\mathfrak{n}},a]f)+[T_{\mathfrak{m}},a](T_{\mathfrak{n}}f),

where the two terms share similar structures and we only show the control of the first one. Using Bony’s paraproduct decomposition in Appendix
D.1
, we rewrite this commutator as

\displaystyle[T_{\mathfrak{n}},a]f=

\displaystyle~T_{\mathfrak{n}}T_{a}f+T_{\mathfrak{n}}T_{f}a+T_{\mathfrak{n}}(R(a,f))-T_{a}T_{\mathfrak{n}}f-T_{T_{\mathfrak{n}}f}a-R(a,T_{\mathfrak{n}}f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~[T_{\mathfrak{n}},T_{a}]f+T_{\mathfrak{n}}T_{f}a-T_{T_{\mathfrak{n}}f}a+T_{\mathfrak{n}}(R(a,f))-R(a,T_{\mathfrak{n}}f)

(7.68)

Here we must
a:=\rho-1
instead of
\rho
because
\rho\gtrsim 1
does not belong to
L^{2}
. This also avoids the loss of
\lambda
-weight in
f=\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi
, as
\rho-1=O(\lambda^{2})
. The last two terms on the right side of (
7.6
) are controlled by using Lemma
D.1

\displaystyle|T_{\mathfrak{n}}(R(a,f))|_{1.5}\lesssim

\displaystyle~|R(a,f)|_{1.5}\lesssim|\rho-1|_{2.5}|f|_{0}\lesssim|\lambda^{2}\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{0},

(7.69)

\displaystyle|R(a,T_{\mathfrak{n}}f)|_{1.5}\lesssim

\displaystyle~|a|_{1.5}|T_{\mathfrak{n}}f|_{0.5}\lesssim|\lambda^{2}\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{0.5}.

(7.70)

Next, we control the commutator
[T_{\mathfrak{n}},T_{a}]f
. Since
{\mathfrak{n}},a
are both function depending on
x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, not a symbol depending on both
x^{\prime}
and the frequency variable
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, we have
a\#{\mathfrak{n}}={\mathfrak{n}}\#a=a{\mathfrak{n}}
and thus

\|T_{a}T_{\mathfrak{n}}-T_{\mathfrak{n}}T_{a}\|_{0.5\to 1.5}\leq\|T_{a}T_{\mathfrak{n}}-T_{{\mathfrak{n}}a}\|_{0.5\to 1.5}+\|T_{\mathfrak{n}}T_{a}-T_{{\mathfrak{n}}a}\|_{0.5\to 1.5}\lesssim M_{1}^{0}({\mathfrak{n}})M_{1}^{0}(a)\lesssim C(|\psi|_{C^{2}})|a|_{W^{1,\infty}},

which leads to

\displaystyle|[T_{\mathfrak{n}},T_{a}]f|_{1.5}\lesssim C(|\psi|_{C^{2}})|a|_{W^{1,\infty}}|f|_{0.5}\lesssim C(|\psi|_{C^{2}})|\lambda^{2}\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{0.5}.

(7.71)

The other two terms in (
7.6
) are controlled in the same way and we only show the control of
T_{\mathfrak{n}}T_{f}a
. Since
{\mathfrak{n}}\in\Sigma^{0}
, it suffices to control
|T_{f}a|_{1.5}
. Using Plancherel’s identity and the definition (
7.27
) of paradifferential operators, we have

\displaystyle|T_{f}a|_{1.5}=|\langle\xi\rangle^{1.5}\widehat{T_{f}a}(\xi)|_{L_{\xi}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}=(2\pi)^{-2}\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\langle\xi\rangle^{1.5}\tilde{\chi}(\xi-\eta,\eta)\hat{f}(\xi-\eta)\phi(\eta)\hat{a}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right|_{L_{\xi}^{2}}.

(7.72)

By definition of
\tilde{\chi}
and
\phi
(see Appendix
D.1
), we know that the integrand is nonzero only if
|\eta|>1
and
|\xi-\eta|<\varepsilon_{2}|\eta|
for some
0<\varepsilon_{2}\ll 1
, which means
\langle\xi\rangle
and
\langle\eta\rangle
are comparable:
(1-\varepsilon_{2})|\eta|\leq|\xi|\leq(1+\varepsilon_{2})|\eta|
. Then, using this,
|\tilde{\chi}|\leq 1,|\phi|\leq 1
and Minkowski’s inequality for integrals, we have

\displaystyle|T_{f}a|_{1.5}\lesssim

\displaystyle~\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{\chi}(\xi-\eta,\eta)\hat{f}(\xi-\eta)\langle\eta\rangle^{1.5}\phi(\eta)\hat{a}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right|_{L_{\xi}^{2}}

\displaystyle(\forall 0<\delta<1)\quad\quad\lesssim

\displaystyle~|\hat{f}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\left|\langle\eta\rangle^{-1-\delta}\langle\eta\rangle^{2.5+\delta}\hat{a}(\eta)\right|_{L_{\eta}^{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle~|\hat{f}|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}|\langle\eta\rangle^{-1-\delta}|_{L_{\eta}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}|\langle\eta\rangle^{2.5+\delta}\hat{a}(\eta)|_{L_{\eta}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\lesssim|f|_{0}|a|_{3}\leq|\lambda^{2}\overline{D_{t}}^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{0}|q|_{3}.

(7.73)

Next, we analyze the commutator
[T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}},\overline{D_{t}}]f
for
f=\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi
. Since
\overline{D_{t}}=\partial_{t}+\overline{v}\cdot\overline{\nabla}
and
\partial_{t}
is a time derivative, we only show the details for the control of
[T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}},\partial_{t}]f
. Expanding this commutator, we have

[T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}},\partial_{t}]f=T_{\mathfrak{m}}([T_{\mathfrak{n}},\partial_{t}]f)+[T_{\mathfrak{m}},\partial_{t}]T_{\mathfrak{n}}f.

Again, these two terms have similar structures, so we only focus on the first one, that is, the control of
|[T_{\mathfrak{n}},\partial_{t}]f|_{1.5}
. We have that
[T_{\mathfrak{n}},\partial_{t}]f=-T_{\partial_{t}{\mathfrak{n}}}f
, so using Lemma
D.1
, we have

\displaystyle|T_{\partial_{t}{\mathfrak{n}}}f|_{1.5}\lesssim|\partial_{t}{\mathfrak{n}}|_{L^{\infty}}|f|_{1.5}\lesssim C(|\overline{\nabla}\psi|_{L^{\infty}})|\overline{\nabla}\psi_{t}|_{L^{\infty}}|\overline{D_{t}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{1.5}\leq P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)).

(7.74)

Next, we analyze the commutator
[\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}},a]f
with
a:=\partial_{3}q
and
f:=T_{\mathfrak{m}}T_{\mathfrak{n}}\partial_{t}^{2}\psi
. Using Lemma
D.5
, we have

\displaystyle\forall s>3,\quad|[\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}^{\frac{1}{2}},a]f|_{0}\lesssim C(|\psi|_{s})|a|_{1.5}|f|_{0.5}\lesssim C(|\psi|_{s})\|q\|_{3}|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{2}\leq P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)).

(7.75)

Also, the term
\mathbf{R}_{\psi}^{RT}:=[\mathfrak{N}_{\psi},\partial_{3}q]\partial_{t}^{2}\psi
is controlled in the same way

\displaystyle\forall s>3,\quad|[\mathfrak{N}_{\psi},\partial_{3}q]\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{0}\lesssim C(|\psi|_{s})|\partial_{3}q|_{1.5}|\partial_{t}^{2}\psi|_{1}\leq P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)).

(7.76)

Finally, we need to establish the
L^{2}(\Sigma)
of
\mathbf{R}_{\psi}^{ST}:=\sigma\mathfrak{N}_{\psi}([\partial_{t}^{2},T_{\mathfrak{H}}]\psi+\partial_{t}^{2}R_{\mathfrak{H}})+\sigma R_{\Lambda}^{\psi}(T_{\mathfrak{H}}\partial_{t}^{2}(\psi)).
The difficulty is that this term simultaneously contains the commutators between a paradifferential operator and
\partial_{t}^{2}
, the time derivatives of
R_{\mathfrak{H}}
which is not explicitly calculated in previous works about incompressible fluids
[
3
,
2
,
63
]
, and the control of remainders for the DtN operator. Among the three terms in
\mathbf{R}_{\psi}^{ST}
, the last one is directly controlled by using Lemma
D.3
and Proposition
7.7

\displaystyle|\sigma R_{\Lambda}^{\psi}(T_{\mathfrak{H}}\partial_{t}^{2}(\psi))|_{0}\lesssim\sigma|T_{\mathfrak{H}}\partial_{t}^{2}(\psi)|_{0}\lesssim C(|\psi|_{W^{1,\infty}})|\psi_{tt}|_{2}\leq P(\mathfrak{E}_{4}(t)).

(7.77)

Next, we control the first term in
\mathbf{R}_{\psi}^{ST}
. In view of Lemma
D.2
, it remains to control
|\sigma[\partial_{t}^{2},T_{\mathfrak{H}}]\psi|_{1}
. Expanding the commutators, we have

[\partial_{t}^{2},T_{\mathfrak{H}}]\psi=T_{\partial_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{H}}\psi+2T_{\partial_{t}\mathfrak{H}}\partial_{t}\psi.

We only analyze the first one as the symbol contains second-order time derivative and
\partial_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{H}\notin C^{2}(\Sigma)
and the second one is directly controlled with the help of Proposition
7.7
. Again, using the definition (
7.27
), Plancherel’s identity and Minkowski’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle|T_{\partial_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{H}}\psi|_{1}=

\displaystyle~\left|\langle\xi\rangle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\chi(\xi-\eta)\widehat{\partial_{t}^{2}\mathfrak{H}}(\xi-\eta)\phi(\eta)\hat{\psi}(\eta)\,\mathrm{d}\eta\right|_{L_{\xi}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}

\displaystyle(\forall 0<\delta<1)\quad\lesssim
